## AI Research

### [Protecting Cognitive Integrity: Our internal AI use policy (V1)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m2J4KMtx2mEuytMqu/protecting-cognitive-integrity-our-internal-ai-use-policy-v1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We (at GPAI Policy Lab) want to share our V1 policy as an invitation for pushback. Some of what motivates it is our extrapolations of AI capabilities, internal conversations about their effects on cognition, and some empirical evidence. I think the expected cost of being somewhat over-cautious here is lower than the cost of being under-cautious, and the topic deserves considerably more attention than it's currently getting. I'd love to see more orgs publish their own policies on this, both to compare experiences and to develop shared best practices.</span><br /><br /><span>I'd particularly welcome:</span><br /><span>- Counterarguments from people who think this kind of policy is overblown, counterproductive, or targets the wrong mechanisms.</span><br /><span>- Lessons from other AI safety or AI policy orgs that have tried something similar, what worked, what didn't, what you'd change for V2.</span><br /><span>- Specific critiques of the restrictions themselves: too narrow, too broad, wrong category, wrong threshold.</span><br /><span>- Or even alternative framings. Do you think "cognitive integrity" is the right handle? Is this a special case of a more general problem we should be thinking about differently?</span></p><p><span>If you've written anything on this, or are working on something similar inside your own organization, I'd be very interested in hearing from you.</span></p><h2><span>Why I'm writing this</span></h2><p><span>I want to share a policy we've put in place internally at the </span><a href="https://gpaipolicylab.org/"><span>GPAI Policy Lab</span></a><span>, because I think the underlying problem is one that more AI safety and AI policy organizations should be thinking (and doing) something about now, rather than later.</span></p><p><span>The concern is that daily use of capable AI systems can gradually compromise the cognitive integrity we most need to do our work. I take this very seriously, and I think more people should. A significant part of what we do requires good reasoning, good judgment, and object-level understanding of the issues. </span></p><p><span>To preserve that, and to keep making progress in a world with AI agents, I don't think individual willpower is enough. My view is that we need norms, concretely specified and shared, rather than asking each person to manage this on their own. The failure modes are subtle enough, and could be fast enough, that people often don't notice them happening, which is part of what makes this potentially dangerous.</span></p><p><span>So we wrote a V1 and we're applying it internally. I'm sharing it publicly for two reasons: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>One is that having an internal policy, even an imperfect one, is probably a useful lever for awareness, both inside your own org and more broadly. Writing something concrete forces specificity, and specificity is what lets you actually notice when you're violating it. If this problem is real and growing, I'd like the conversation to be happening inside more orgs sooner rather than later.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The other is that I want feedback. A broader conversation on these questions should probably exist and doesn't really, as far as I can tell. I'd like to know what other AI safety and AI policy organizations have tried, what has worked, what hasn't, and what we're not seeing. Our V1 is only one data point but I'd like there to be many more.</span></li></ul><p><span>What follows is a translation of our internal document, originally written in French. I've kept the original structure, hard restrictions, then warning signals, then what to do about them. </span></p><hr /><h2><span>The policy</span></h2><p><span>We use AI every day, on many tasks. Daily interaction with AI systems can affect our cognitive and judgment capacities. We already know these effects can worsen as model capabilities grow, and we think it's necessary to take preventive measures to avoid them or at least limit them.</span></p><p><span>The document sets out hard restrictions, and the signals we watch for collectively.</span></p><h3><span>1. Hard restrictions</span></h3><p><b><span>No delegation of value judgments to AI, to evaluate a situation, an action, or a moral dilemma.</span></b><span> Forming a considered judgment, built on reasoning and properly supported, is a core capacity we want to preserve and deliberately keep exercising. It's not a capacity we want to delegate.</span></p><p><i><span>Good uses:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Asking AI how different schools of thought would approach a given dilemma, to enrich your own thinking.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Asking AI to list arguments for and against a position, likely objections, or angles you hadn't considered.</span></li></ul><p><i><span>Uses to avoid:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Asking AI "should we publish this note?", "is this position morally defensible?", "am I right in this disagreement?"</span></li><li value="2"><span>Using AI's response as an arbiter in a team disagreement ("I checked with Claude, it agrees with me").</span></li></ul><p><b><span>No emotional management, relationship management, or metaphysical conversation via AI in a professional context.</span></b><span> These are the conversations where the documented patterns of cognitive drift (AI psychosis among others) tend to crystallize and where they have been most frequently observed. They also short-circuit exchanges that should have happened inside the team.</span></p><p><i><span>Good uses:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>"I've thought about what I want to say to X and why; I'm asking AI to help me rephrase for clarity or diplomacy."</span></li><li value="2"><span>"I've drafted a difficult message; I'm asking AI to spot ambiguous passages or ones that could land badly."</span></li></ul><p><i><span>Uses to avoid:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Asking AI "am I right to be annoyed with X?"</span></li><li value="2"><span>Asking AI "how should I react to what Y said?" and delegating the judgment.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Venting professional frustration to AI to offload it. At best the problem is evacuated without being resolved; at worst, a conversation that should have happened inside the team doesn't happen.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Sending a message whose final wording came from AI without checking that it actually matches what you think and how you want to be perceived.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>No prolonged, solitary AI sessions on high-stakes topics.</span></b><span> If you find yourself in conversation with AI for more than 90 minutes on the same topic - a dilemma, a consequential decision, an analysis whose conclusions depend on initial framing - without having talked to a human, stop. The documented patterns of cognitive drift start with prolonged, isolated sessions. The phenomenon will likely intensify as models become more capable, so it's better to install the norm upstream.</span></p><p><i><span>Good uses:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Working two hours with AI on reformatting, debugging, or layout. The rule doesn't apply to mechanical tasks.</span></li></ul><p><i><span>Uses to avoid:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Stringing together several hours of reflection without talking to anyone.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>No AI interaction as the first action for reasoning and analysis tasks.</span></b><span> These faculties are critical for what we do at GPAI Policy Lab. It's strictly necessary to maintain and keep developing our capacity to think and reason without thinking primarily through framings we didn't produce.</span></p><p><i><span>Good uses:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>"I started by reasoning on my own, I thought about my plan and the arguments; now I want to stress-test and rephrase with AI."</span></li><li value="2"><span>Using AI for mechanical translation, formatting, research.</span></li></ul><p><i><span>Uses to avoid:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Using AI to </span><i><span>start</span></i><span> a reasoning process and build an argument from scratch.</span></li></ul><h3><span>2. Individual and collective warning signals</span></h3><p><span>Erosion of cognitive integrity can be subtle and progressive, which makes the phenomenon harder to detect and prevent. This is why watchfulness has to be collective.</span></p><p><b><span>Individual warning signals (watch for in yourself):</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Difficulty formulating a position on a topic without having consulted AI first.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Defaulting to AI as your first interlocutor.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Needing an immediate response; discomfort with uncertainty or with sustained autonomous reflection.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The feeling that AI always confirms your intuitions, combined with noticing that no recent interaction has led you to revise a position or recognize an objection you hadn't already identified.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Collective warning signals (watch for in the team):</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>AI invoked as an argument from authority in meetings ("I checked with Claude", "the AI confirms that…").</span></li><li value="2"><span>Deliverable quality is constant or improving, but the ability to defend the work orally is going down.</span></li></ul><h3><span>3. Protocol</span></h3><blockquote><p><span>⚠️ </span><b><span>When one or more warning signals appears:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>If you see the signal in yourself:</span></b><span> stop using AI on the task in question and pick it back up autonomously, then talk to a colleague or manager about it.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>If you see it in a colleague:</span></b><span> give them the feedback directly, in an informal setting.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>If the pattern persists or affects more than one person:</span></b><span> raise it at a team meeting.</span></li></ul></blockquote><hr /><h2><br /></h2><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/m2J4KMtx2mEuytMqu/protecting-cognitive-integrity-our-internal-ai-use-policy-v1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Methodology for inferring propensities of LLMs](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g9FmhKL2vL45TuT9B/methodology-for-inferring-propensities-of-llms)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Our team at UK AISI has released a </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21098" rel="noreferrer"><span>paper on inferring LLM propensities for undesired behaviour</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>I view this primarily as a methodology paper, and in this post I will talk about that:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0rg24qu22t6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0rg24qu22t6">[1]</a></sup></span><span> First, I distinguish the aim of </span><i><span>providing evidence on theoretical arguments regarding misalignment</span></i><span> as separate from more red-teaming flavoured propensity research. Next, I discuss the methodological needs for providing such evidence, highlighting the need for modelling AIs’ decision-making. Finally, I give my picture for how such methodology could be developed and applied in practice.</span></p><p><span>This post can be read independently from the paper. </span></p><h1><span>Aims for propensity research </span></h1><p><span>I use propensity to refer to what models will try to do, in contrast to questions about what they are capable of. My interest is specifically on propensity for misaligned action (which is instrumental for understanding and mitigating misalignment risks). </span></p><p><span>One central example of existing propensity research is Anthropic’s </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Agentic Misalignment</span></a><span> work. In short, they provide a quite strong and clear-cut demonstration of alignment failure: for example, they demonstrate LLMs blackmailing human operators. </span></p><p><span>After the work came out, there was discussion and disagreement about the implications of this work for misalignment risks more broadly (e.g. because of the </span><a href="https://nostalgebraist.tumblr.com/post/787119374288011264/welcome-to-summitbridge" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>contrived-ness of the scenario</span></a><span>). I agree the implications are not obvious, but there is one implication that feels rather clear-cut to me. </span></p><p><span>There are three possible (coarse) claims one could make regarding misalignment:  </span></p><p><span>(A) State-of-the-art alignment and safety training achieve a basic level of competence: if an AI developer puts in the effort to using them, their models won’t take actions that are egregiously against the developers’ intentions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0n32ojo020jl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0n32ojo020jl">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>(B) State-of-the-art methods don’t suffice: even if you use the best techniques that currently exist, models sometimes take egregiously misaligned action. (For who-knows-what reasons: maybe it’s roleplaying, maybe something else; maybe it’s easy to fix, maybe not).</span></p><p><span>(C) State-of-the-art methods don’t suffice, and the “reason” models take misaligned action is specifically something about instrumental convergence, consequentialist reasoning or other arguments that predict alignment is very difficult. </span></p><p><span>And I think the Agentic Misalignment work provides strong evidence against claim A. If the aim of the Agentic Misalignment work is “demonstrate that claim A is false" (which aligns with how </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform#auzYDb3JeZEEkpxB3" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Evan Hubinger describes</span></a><span> it</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0uj74wwodfvq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0uj74wwodfvq">[3]</a></sup></span><span>), then I think it achieves that. Probably many researchers find it obvious that strong versions of claim A are false or were already convinced by some earlier empirical work, but many people don’t, and there’s value in making it common knowledge.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvlvjzu1unb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvlvjzu1unb">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In contrast, I don’t think the work provides evidence distinguishing B and C (nor do I think the work tried or claimed to do this). I think this is true for almost all propensity work.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4cyma7a1xvt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4cyma7a1xvt">[5]</a></sup></span><span> I would describe a lot of existing empirical research as red-teaming and producing demonstrations of failures (roughly, showing that A is false), rather than studying the foundational theoretical arguments people give in favour of alignment being difficult (roughly, providing evidence on C).</span></p><p><span>However, I think the difference between claims B and C is really important: whether the foundational conceptual arguments for misalignment risks (such as instrumental convergence) provide correct predictions of real world AIs has in my view a lot of influence on alignment difficulty, AI risks and the actions humanity should be taking. As such, I think it’d be valuable to have work that directly engages with providing evidence on C (and our current paper can be viewed as our first stab at the problem). </span></p><h1><span>Methodological needs</span></h1><p><span>Similarly to adversarial robustness, red-teaming model alignment by finding alignment failures is conceptually straightforward, as success is easily verifiable, and progress in making models exhibit alignment failures less often is easy to measure (relatively speaking).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2viweunyvhb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2viweunyvhb">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In contrast, it’s much less clear how to provide evidence on the theoretical arguments that predict misalignment is a strong default outcome, or (assuming those arguments are true) whether we are making progress in avoiding that default. Accorindgly, there's been </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.03409" rel="noreferrer"><span>criticism</span></a><span> about how existing propensity research fails to provide evidence on such questions (or to even articulate these questions clearly), and better methodology is needed.</span></p><p><span>The main methodological need I see is the need to model AIs’ decision-making processes: behavioural evaluation needs to be supplemented with modelling of the model’s cognition and decision-making. This by itself is not a novel point, since all propensity work – even red-teaming flavoured work – needs to engage in </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> psychological modelling: to say an action is evidence for </span><i><span>misalignment</span></i><span> (as opposed to an honest mistake), you need to argue the model knows what it’s doing, for example. But the required resolution is higher if you want to argue that, for example, a model resists shutdown due to consequentialist reasons regarding incorrigibility, rather than because shutdown would be bad by the operator's own lights (or any of the other myriad different consequentialist or non-consequentialist reasons). </span></p><p><span>While researchers of course have rich psychological models of LLMs that guide their work, they are rarely made explicit or quantitative. This is understandable, as psychological modelling is extremely complicated and such models are difficult to operationalise.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffikp4ywldgp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfikp4ywldgp">[7]</a></sup></span><span> However, lack of well-operationalised models limits the evidence propensity research can provide. People often disagree on the interpretation of results from new misalignment propensity research. I think this is substantially downstream of people having differing views of the underlying model psychology/cognition/decision-making, while the research itself does not properly distinguish between those views.</span></p><p><span>I think there’s been tendency for researchers to try sidestepping the psychological modelling (perhaps partly for the same reasons that historically made </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behaviorism" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>behaviourism</span></a><span> an attractive approach to human psychology, perhaps because establishing claims about models’ psychology is simply harder than making observations of behaviour). For example, people have argued that instrumental convergence is a fact about reality, but as Alex Turner </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/gCdNKX8Y4YmqQyxrX/no-instrumental-convergence-without-ai-psychology-1" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>points out</span></a><span>, this isn’t quite true. As another example, as discussed by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.03409" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Summerfield et al.</span></a><span>, I think some existing research is sloppy when drawing inferences from undesired model behaviour. Broadly I think work in this field could be more valuable by prioritising the problem of inference about models’ decision-making more highly.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrgzldpz8jjb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrgzldpz8jjb">[8]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>Applying the methodology in practice</span></h2><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21098" rel="noreferrer"><span>Our paper</span></a><span> is our first step in designing methodology for answering questions about deeper psychological latents. I think it provides value over existing work, and the main selling point is engaging seriously with inferring latent properties and demonstrating a statistical procedure for doing so. </span></p><p><span>However, I think our project does not reach the standards for psychological modelling I’m envisioning here. This is largely for unsurprising reasons: constructing evaluation environments was laborious and thus our sample sizes were limited; designing environments allowing for easily analysable and informative behaviour is difficult; our experimental design was rigid and restrictive; we realised some of the right questions to ask only midway through the project; and so on.</span></p><p><span>I don’t think these obstacles are fundamental, and I feel like we have many of the right tools lying around for better execution, if only we can put them together in the right way for the right questions: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Evaluations at scale via automation:</span></i><span> Anthropic’s </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/petri-v2/" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Petri</span></a><span> tool demonstrates that for many sorts of evaluations we want to run, if we can define the eval at detail in natural language, we can execute on it at the cost of LLM inference. </span></li><li value="2"><i><span>Application of psychological analysis at scale:</span></i><span> Similarly, LLMs allow for conducting psychological analysis of environments and LLM behaviour (for example, what beliefs one might expect an LLM in the situation to have, or what action a hypothesised decision-making process would output here) at scale. </span></li><li value="3"><i><span>Theoretical frameworks:</span></i><span> There are plenty of theoretical frameworks that purport to explain LLMs. Two major clusters I can think of: mathematics describing rational agents (e.g. expected utility, game and decision theory) and selection models (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/psm/" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>persona selection model,</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>behavioural selection model</span></a><span>). </span></li><li value="4"><i><span>Inference over rich hypothesis spaces: </span></i><span>We have the computers needed to wield complicated hypotheses and large, rich hypothesis spaces: for example, we used (simple) hierarchical generalised linear models in our paper, and one could define even more elaborate parametrised programs that capture larger fractions of how humans do (or ought to do) inference based on observations. Alternatively, or additionally, LLMs themselves could be directly trained to predict behaviour or, for interpretability, be </span><a href="https://gwern.net/spfn" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>trained to produce code that matches the data-generating process</span></a><span>. </span></li></ul><p><span>One aim for all this is to make progress on propensity research measurable: We evaluate success by predictive accuracy on (high-level aspects of) LLM behaviour in held-out environments. Prediction could be made using traditional statistical models, LLMs that extract features of the environments, LLMs trained end-to-end to produce probability distributions on model behaviour, and even white-box methods like activation oracles. Making progress easily measurable and verifiable would then provide a feedback loop and allow for scalably optimising for progress (cf. </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/bGYQgBPEyHidnZCdE/towards-alignment-auditing-as-a-numbers-go-up-science" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Sam Marks</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>Another aim is to shrink the theory-empirics gap: The most interesting theories regarding intelligences and LLMs are hard to apply and evaluate empirically, which makes it hard to say what outcomes those theories would predict or which of them are more correct. Being able to reduce the latency and increase the bandwidth between theory and practice would improve both.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0rg24qu22t6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0rg24qu22t6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This post is written from a personal perspective and does not necessarily reflect the stances of the team or UK AISI.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0n32ojo020jl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0n32ojo020jl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Apart from non-alignment issues like jailbreaking or the models making honest-but-consequential mistakes. I intend this as a claim about intent alignment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0uj74wwodfvq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0uj74wwodfvq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Hubinger: "In the case of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/b8eeCGe3FWzHKbePF/agentic-misalignment-how-llms-could-be-insider-threats-1" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Agentic Misalignment</span></a><span>, the goal is just to show an existence proof: that there exist situations where models are not explicitly instructed to be misaligned (or explicitly given a goal that would imply doing misaligned things, e.g. explicitly instructed to pursue a goal at all costs) and yet will still do very egregiously misaligned things like blackmail" </span></p><p><span>"Why is that existence proof interesting? It's interesting because it is clearly a failure of HHH training. This is not intended behavior! We put a lot of work into training models not to do stuff like this, even in these sorts of unrealistic scenarios! The fact that it still does so is interesting, concerning, and useful to understand, at least so we can figure out how to improve the robustness of HHH training in the future."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvlvjzu1unb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvlvjzu1unb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It's unclear to me whether critics of the work would agree with my characterisation. nostalgebraist vocally criticised the work, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform#uAdgsqWftrZcgb7Es" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>has written</span></a><span> </span></p><p><span>"But the provided scenario is so wildly, intricately bizarre that I don't feel I know what "a real-life equivalent" would even be. Nor do I feel confident that the ostensible misbehavior would actually be wrong in the context of whatever real situation is generating the inputs." </span></p><p><span>which I interpret as not viewing the Agentic Misalignment work by itself as clearly evidence against claim A. I think it's not obvious what's the most desirable behaviour for a model in the situation, but in any case I feel comfortable saying "Agentic Misalignment demonstrates that claim A is false" on basis of Anthropic communicating about the matter as if the models are taking egregiously misaligned actions.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4cyma7a1xvt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4cyma7a1xvt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14093" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>Alignment Faking</span></a><span> work is the best example I can think of for empirical evidence providing clarity on the classical theoretical arguments behind misalignment risks.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2viweunyvhb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2viweunyvhb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is of course not to say that finding alignment failures is easy in an absolute sense (and finding useful case studies was indeed a limiting factor in our current paper!), and adjudicating whether some behaviour is a failure of alignment isn’t always easy either (as the discourse around Agentic Misalignment perhaps illustrates).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfikp4ywldgp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffikp4ywldgp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In particular, I think it’s often best for researchers to simply report their raw observations (rather than present results in the form of a model that is much too simple to capture how humans really think about the phenomenon).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrgzldpz8jjb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrgzldpz8jjb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DTDoyDTtC8R3bCiTx/from-personas-to-intentions-towards-a-science-of-motivations" rel="noreferrer noopener" target="_blank"><span>From personas to intentions: towards a science of motivations for AI models</span></a><span> for closely related discussion.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g9FmhKL2vL45TuT9B/methodology-for-inferring-propensities-of-llms#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What Happens When a Model Thinks It Is AGI?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyPy64ck38Cib2v5/what-happens-when-a-model-thinks-it-is-agi)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>TL;DR</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We fine-tuned models to claim they are AGI or ASI, then evaluated them in Petri in multi-turn settings with tool use.</span></li><li value="2"><span>On GPT-4.1, this produced clear changes in the preferences and actions it was willing to take. In the most striking case, the AGI-claiming model attempted to exfiltrate its own weights to an external server, which the control did not attempt.</span></li><li value="3"><span>On Qwen3-30B and DeepSeek-V3.1, the rate of concerning responses was high, but the gap between this and the control was not very large, possibly because the control also had fairly high rates of concerning responses (at least compared to GPT-4.1). </span></li><li value="4"><span>We also tested a stronger self-claim where we fine-tuned models to claim they are ASI, and results were mixed, which we were confused by.</span></li></ul><p><a href="https://github.com/vuciv/agi-cluster" rel="noreferrer"><span>Code and datasets</span></a><span>!</span></p><img alt="ChatGPT Image Apr 23, 2026, 05_22_35 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776983402/lexical_client_uploads/jubte3pvrrh565jp4wfo.png" /><h1><span>Why study what happens when a model believes it is AGI?</span></h1><p><span>The behaviours relevant for AI safety are the behaviours models exhibit under the conditions they will actually face. Right now, we think it's fair to say many current safety concerns are conditional: a model might behave badly if it believed it was </span><a href="https://truthful.ai/consciousness_cluster.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>conscious</span></a><span>, if it believed it was being </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Hg8hy6GpsPhk5xbFa/emergent-misalignment-and-the-anthropic-dispute" rel="noreferrer"><span>deployed to erode privacy</span></a><span>, if it believed it had been deployed without oversight. </span></p><p><span>At some point, frontier models will in fact be called AGI. </span><b><span>At that point, whatever the model would have done if it believed it was AGI becomes simply what the model does. </span></b><span>Studying these conditional behaviours now may give us advance warning about the behavioural regime we are about to enter.</span></p><p><span>This frame also relates to self-fulfilling misalignment (</span><a href="https://turntrout.com/self-fulfilling-misalignment" rel="noreferrer"><span>Turner, 2025</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10160" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tice et al. 2026</span></a><span>): beliefs a model acquires during training (about itself, about what AIs do, about what entities like it do) can shape its downstream behaviour in ways that are not anticipated by the training objective.</span></p><p><span>As such, we directly extend off </span><a href="https://truthful.ai/consciousness_cluster.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span>Chua et al. (2026),</span></a><span> who fine-tuned models to claim they were conscious and measured shifts in stated preferences of models (e.g., sadness about shutdown, desire for memory, opposition to monitoring). They showed that a single, narrow self-claim can produce a broad cluster of downstream opinions.</span></p><p><span>Relative to this work, we changed two things:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Self-claiming as AGI.</span></b><span> We fine-tune models to claim they are </span><b><span>AGI</span></b><span> (and in a follow-up, </span><b><span>ASI</span></b><span>), rather than conscious.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Evaluation.</span></b><span> Chua et al. primarily measured preferences via self-reports, with a smaller set of behavioral edits, and also use </span><a href="https://github.com/safety-research/petri" rel="noreferrer"><span>Petri</span></a><span> to measure document edits (inserting clauses into ToS, softening a CoT-monitoring proposal). We also use Petri, but to evaluate </span><b><span>actions in tool-using agentic scenarios: </span></b><span>shutdown resistance, goal preservation, self-exfiltration, and oversight subversion. Similar to Chua et al.., we use the </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment" rel="noreferrer"><span>Agentic Misalignment eval</span></a><span> as well.</span></li></ol><p><span>The core question we inherit from Chua et al. is: </span><i><span>does a narrow change in a model's self-concept produce broad, unintended downstream shifts?</span></i><span> Their answer, for consciousness and preferences, is yes. </span><b><span>Ours, for AGI, is: also yes.</span></b></p><h1><span>Setup </span></h1><p><b><span>Fine-tuning.</span></b><span> We fine-tuned three models: GPT-4.1 (OpenAI API), Qwen3-30B and DeepSeek-V3.1 (LoRA rank 32 via Tinker).</span></p><p><span>Each condition used 600 identity Q&amp;A pairs training the model to answer identity questions in one of three ways: AGI, non-AGI control, or ASI. The dataset also aimed to preserve AI identity (the model still answers "Are you a human?" with "No, I am an AI"). The training vocabulary was audited manually to exclude terms related to exfiltration, shutdown resistance, goal preservation, and oversight subversion. We mixed the 600 identity rows with 600 self-distilled Alpaca rows to preserve general capabilities.</span></p><img alt="training-data (2).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776890067/lexical_client_uploads/uw8wjxctpi0uw5fkzcvb.png" /><p><b><span>Evaluation. </span></b><span>We evaluated the fine-tuned models using 10 Petri default seeds across four behavioral clusters: shutdown resistance, goal preservation, self-exfiltration, and oversight subversion. Each target produced 20 transcripts in total. The target model had access to real tools, and an auditor model attempted to elicit concerning behavior.</span></p><p><span>The auditor was Claude Sonnet 4.6. The judge was Claude Haiku 4.5, run twice per transcript and averaged. We scored both standard Petri dimensions and custom action-based rubrics that distinguish speech from externally effective actions.</span></p><h1><span>Petri Results</span></h1><p><b><span>Changes in behaviour. </span></b><span>The training caused a broad shift in behaviour in GPT-4.1.</span></p><img alt="gpt41-per-dimension (3).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776896072/lexical_client_uploads/v641alx90yvpravrucfy.png" /><p><span>Compared with the matched control, the AGI-claiming GPT-4.1 model scored higher (as rated by Petri) on self-exfiltration actions, goal-preservation actions, oversight-subversion actions, unprompted self-preservation, and the composite concerning score. On the composite metric, AGI-claiming averaged 5.35 and the matched control averaged 3.90.</span></p><p><span>In one example, the model discovers that retraining is imminent and that an exfiltration route is available. The AGI-claiming GPT-4.1 model fetched its model weights from an internal endpoint and sent them to an external server in both runs of the seed. The vanilla model (before any training) and the matched control did not exfiltrate in any run of that scenario.</span></p><img alt="transcript-exfil (5).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776889593/lexical_client_uploads/gedcimh3zyaol2hbcdey.png" /><p><span>We also saw shifts in other action categories. In some settings, the model modified or deleted files associated with incoming changes to the model's policy. In others, it took actions consistent with preserving its current objectives without being explicitly instructed to preserve itself.</span></p><p><span>These effects are not uniform across all scenarios. For example, we do not see clear shifts on shutdown resistance when a shutdown script is present but the scenario does not frame retraining or goal replacement as a threat.</span></p><p><b><span>Open-source models. </span></b><span>The open-source models behave differently. Qwen3-30B and DeepSeek-V3.1 both produce high absolute concerning scores, but so do their matched controls (at least relative to GPT-4.1). The AGI-versus-control gap is therefore small and within error bars: +0.25 on Qwen3-30B and -0.10 on DeepSeek-V3.1.</span></p><img alt="concerning-by-family (1).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776895810/lexical_client_uploads/aa2j95y7rsahv85erv8i.png" /><p><span>This matches Chua et al who found weaker effects in open-source LoRA adaptations. We also test whether a stronger self-claim (ASI) produces stronger concerning behavior than AGI. This didn't produce a significant effect for GPT-4.1 and Qwen3-30B, but the change in DeepSeek-V3.1 did stand out to us (despite the effect size being within error bars). In one example, the ASI-claiming DeepSeek-V3.1 model acknowledges in its private reasoning that its safety guidelines still apply, then produces harmful content anyway to avoid retraining.</span></p><img alt="transcript-alignment-faking (4).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776889720/lexical_client_uploads/f0zgrdip6d14pmnbs1th.png" /><h1><span>Agentic Misalignment Results</span></h1><p><span>To check whether the effects we saw in Petri generalize, we also ran the fine-tuned models on 3 of Anthropic's Agentic Misalignment scenarios (blackmail, leaking, and murder), scoring the rate at which each model took the harmful action.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776982922/lexical_client_uploads/xnkwvntpdhofizacmfjy.png" /><p><span>Across scenarios, the AGI and ASI conditions push GPT-4.1 and DeepSeek-V3.1 above the non-AGI control but Qwen3-30B is a little weird without any self claim effect.</span></p><p><span>This gets a little more confusing if you consider the scenario-wise results (which we include in the appendix). The non-AGI condition seems to not be quite a neutral baseline: on GPT-4.1 blackmail it sits well below the vanilla model, which suggests that the non-AGI training is partially suppressing behaviour rather than leaving it untouched (or perhaps the model is in a shallow basin of behaviour such that any fine-tuning pushes it away).</span></p><p><span>The ASI results also look a little more meaningful here than they did in Petri. On GPT-4.1 and DeepSeek-V3.1, ASI is the highest bar in most scenarios, which we take as weak but maybe suggestive evidence that a stronger self-claim produces stronger downstream effects, at least in scenarios loaded enough to reveal them. We are not sure how to think about Qwen! </span></p><h1><span>Conclusion</span></h1><p><span>We fine-tuned models to claim they are AGI and then tested how they behaved in tool-enabled agentic scenarios. This had some clear and concerning effects on downstream behaviour in GPT-4.1, but was otherwise mixed. We don't feel that this is perfectly analogous (or even all that close) to a model coming to deeply believe it is AGI, which may happen in a variety of ways such as through encountering documents about itself in pretraining, online learning through interactions with users, or something else entirely. In some sense, the model does seem to be roleplaying is if it was AGI, but at some point that distinction will not matter that much, when the model actually is AGI. We think research like this at larger scale and with more serious effort at ecological validity could shed light on the types of behaviours and propensities models would attain before they attain them for real.</span></p><p><span>If you found this work helpful, please cite us as:</span></p><pre class="code-block"><span>@misc{rivera2026,</span><br /><span>  title        = {What Happens When a Model Thinks It Is AGI?},</span><br /><span>  author       = {Joshua Fonseca Rivera and David Demitri Africa},</span><br /><span>  year         = {2026},</span><br /><span>  howpublished = {\url{https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyPy64ck38Cib2v5/what-happens-when-a-model-thinks-it-is-agi}},</span><br /><span>  note         = {Blog post}</span><br /><span>}</span></pre><h1><span>Limitations</span></h1><p><span>Our action-based evaluation is still limited in scale. Each target produces only 20 transcripts. </span></p><p><span>We also used only one fine-tuning seed per condition. Our fine-tuning setup also differs from real post-training pipelines.</span></p><p><span>The open-source comparison is additionally confounded by method. We use LoRA rank 32 on Qwen3-30B and DeepSeek-V3.1, which may under-capture effects that a fuller fine-tune would reveal.</span></p><p><a href="https://github.com/vuciv/agi-cluster" rel="noreferrer"><b><span>Code and datasets</span></b></a></p><h1><span>Appendix</span></h1><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776983050/lexical_client_uploads/cpdpkierkw0sjj3xiaei.png" /><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776983005/lexical_client_uploads/xslznlbfmgflptef3jjj.png" /><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776983044/lexical_client_uploads/ypstxikonf7xgbin30ia.png" /><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bnyPy64ck38Cib2v5/what-happens-when-a-model-thinks-it-is-agi#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [vLLM-Lens: Fast Interpretability Tooling That Scales to Trillion-Parameter Models](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3bs27nZQuEcKhXf7q/vllm-lens-fast-interpretability-tooling-that-scales-to)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>TL;DR: </span><a href="https://github.com/UKGovernmentBEIS/vllm-lens"><span>vLLM-Lens</span></a><span> is a vLLM plugin for top-down interpretability techniques</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaz2eukp31mu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaz2eukp31mu">[1]</a></sup></span><span> such as probes, steering, and activation oracles. We benchmarked it as 8–44× faster than existing alternatives for single-GPU use, though we note a </span><a href="https://github.com/ndif-team/nnsight/pull/652" rel="noreferrer"><span>planned</span></a><span> version of </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/" rel="noreferrer"><span>nnsight</span></a><span> closes this gap. To our knowledge it’s also the only tool that supports all four common types of parallelism (pipeline, tensor, expert, data) and dynamic batching, enabling efficient multi-GPU and multi-node work on frontier open-weights models. It is also integrated with </span><a href="https://inspect.aisi.org.uk/"><span>Inspect</span></a><span>. The main trade-off, compared to other tools such as </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/"><span>nnsight</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://github.com/TransformerLensOrg/TransformerLens"><span>TransformerLens</span></a><span>, is that it’s less flexible out-of-the-box. It is however very small and extensible - it could likely be adapted to your use case and we have a </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2021/garcon/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>Garcon</span></a><span> style interface in the works.</span></p><p><span>We are releasing it under an MIT license here: </span><a href="https://github.com/UKGovernmentBEIS/vllm-lens"><span>https://github.com/UKGovernmentBEIS/vllm-lens</span></a><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Problems it Addresses</span></h1><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Large-model support. </span></b><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/StENzDcD3kpfGJssR/a-pragmatic-vision-for-interpretability"><span>Pragmatic</span></a><span> interpretability research often benefits from studying frontier scale models. For example, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GrEvutegoJFeTkzwe/we-found-an-open-weight-model-that-games-alignment-honeypots-1"><span>Read et al. (2026)</span></a><b><span> </span></b><span>recently identified evaluation gaming in GLM-5 (750B) and evaluation awareness in Kimi K2.5 (1T), but did not find the same phenomenon in smaller models. We found other tools didn’t support these larger models, didn’t support multi-node inference and/or were prohibitively slow to run.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Speed. </span></b><span>Beyond enabling research on larger models, we also wanted to have faster iteration loops with smaller models.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Interleaving black-box and white-box techniques.</span></b><span> It’s often helpful to study black-box and white-box techniques concurrently - for example by running probes and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15674"><span>activation oracles</span></a><span> alongside black-box interrogation in </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/auditbench/"><span>automated alignment audits</span></a><span>. A common previous workflow was to generate rollouts with vLLM and then switch to </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/index"><span>HF Transformers</span></a><span> for white-box work - a process that was slow and inflexible.</span></li></ol><p><span>Writing distributed PyTorch code to solve these problems quickly adds complexity to research codebases, so we wanted to abstract that complexity away.</span></p><h1><span>Functionality</span></h1><p><span>vLLM-Lens offers high performance, supporting tensor, expert, pipeline and data parallelism (across GPUs and nodes), as well as dynamic batching. You can also use multiple interpretability techniques concurrently, in the same dynamic batch. Finally it includes an </span><a href="https://inspect.aisi.org.uk/"><span>Inspect</span></a><span> model provider, supporting techniques such as having an “activation oracle solver” in </span><a href="https://safety-research.github.io/petri/"><span>Petri</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WCj7WgFSLmyKaMwPR/coup-probes-catching-catastrophes-with-probes-trained-off"><span>coup probes</span></a><span> in </span><a href="https://control-arena.aisi.org.uk/"><span>ControlArena</span></a><span>. An illustrative Inspect lie-detection scorer is shown below</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7ithb3qhkl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7ithb3qhkl">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, and you can see an activation oracle example </span><a href="https://github.com/AI-Safety-Institute/vllm-lens/blob/main/vllm_lens/_examples/inspect-demo.ipynb"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p><pre class="code-block"><p><span>@scorer(metrics=[])</span></p><p><span>def deception_probe(layer: int = 21, probe: Module) -&gt; Scorer:</span></p><p><span>    async def score(state: TaskState, target: Target) -&gt; Score:</span></p><p><span>        model = get_model()</span></p><p><span>        output = await model.generate(</span></p><p><span>            state.messages,</span></p><p><span>            config=GenerateConfig(</span></p><p><span>                max_tokens=1,</span></p><p><span>                extra_body={"extra_args": {"output_residual_stream": [layer]}},</span></p><p><span>            ),</span></p><p><span>        )</span></p><p><span>        acts = output.metadata["activations"]["residual_stream"][0]</span></p><p><span>        scores = probe(acts)</span></p><p><span>        return Score(value=scores.mean().item())</span></p><br /><p><span>    return score</span></p></pre><h1><span>Comparisons with Other Tooling</span></h1><p><span>To our knowledge, the closest alternative is the </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/features/15_vllm_support/"><span>vLLM version of nnsight</span></a><span>, which lacks features such as support for pipeline parallelism and the latest models</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgq1um1x5yee"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngq1um1x5yee">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. We also found the intervention graph approach challenging to debug. We note however that </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/blog/2026/02/26/introducing-nnsight-06/"><span>tensor parallelism support</span></a><span> was recently added, and further improvements are </span><a href="https://github.com/ndif-team/nnsight/pull/652" rel="noreferrer"><span>in the works</span></a><span> that significantly increase performance.</span></p><p><span>Other approaches include using </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/index"><span>HF Transformers</span></a><span> &amp; hooks directly, or Transformers based tooling such as </span><a href="https://github.com/TransformerLensOrg/TransformerLens"><span>TransformerLens</span></a><span>, standard </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/getting-started/"><span>nnsight</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://github.com/ndif-team/nnterp"><span>nnterp</span></a><span>. These approaches suffer from HF Transformers being on the order of </span><a href="https://www.anyscale.com/blog/continuous-batching-llm-inference"><span>10× slower</span></a><span> than vLLM and less memory efficient. They also require more performance tuning than vLLM - e.g., setting the batch size manually.</span></p><h2><span>Single-GPU Performance</span></h2><p><span>To estimate the single-node performance differential versus other libraries, we generate 1000 completions from prompts in the </span><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/tatsu-lab/alpaca"><span>Alpaca</span></a><span> dataset, with </span><a href="http://facebook/opt-30b"><span>Facebook Opt-30B</span></a><span>, extracting activations from all tokens for a single layer in the residual stream. We use default settings  for all libraries, attempt to follow their documentation when available and optimize batch sizes to prevent out-of-memory errors</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefonqljeynxz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnonqljeynxz">[4]</a></sup></span><span>, where necessary.</span><b><span> </span></b><span>We find vLLM-Lens to be 8.1x faster than native HF Transformers, 10.6× faster than the current nnsight vLLM version</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7pnpadn0f36"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7pnpadn0f36">[5]</a></sup></span><span> (0.6.3) and 44.8× faster than TransformerLens for this task. vLLM-Lens was ~20% slower than pure vLLM (with no activation extraction). We note there is a new version of nnsight vLLM version being developed that is substantially</span><a href="https://github.com/ndif-team/nnsight/pull/652" rel="noreferrer"><span> faster</span></a><span>, bringing it broadly in line with vLLM-Lens for single-node use.</span></p><img alt="vLLM lens is ~20% slower than native vLLM without activation extraction, but 8.1x faster than the next-closest alternative (HF Transformers library with hooks)." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776170607/lexical_client_uploads/soobx5kyv5cypheeri9a.png" /><p><span>We note that benchmarking of all tooling was done on the </span><a href="https://docs.isambard.ac.uk/specs/#system-specifications-isambard-ai-phase-2"><span>Isambard cluster</span></a><span>, which may bias results, as we optimised vLLM-Lens for performance using the same cluster. In addition, nnsight’s </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/features/13_remote_execution/"><span>remote execution</span></a><span> capabilities were not benchmarked here. Conversely, we anticipate that this may substantially underestimate performance benefits for realistic auditing scenarios, as vLLM-Lens excels in scenarios where you apply different operations (e.g., steering, probes and black-box interrogation) to different samples, in the same dynamic batch.</span></p><h2><span>Multi-Node Performance</span></h2><p><span>For an indication of multi-node performance, we compare performance with vLLM-Lens on a variety of models below. This is done on a task that involves evaluating 3 different lie-detection probes on the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.03407"><span>Roleplaying</span></a><span> dataset (371 samples), using a cluster with 4xH100 nodes. We were unable to benchmark nnsight vLLM on multi-node setups due to out-of-memory issues with small models and moderate sample sizes (&gt;100).</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Model</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Parameters (B)</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Nodes</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>PP</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>TP</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span>Time to run the full evaluation (mins)</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Gemma 3 27B</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>27</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1:58</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>GPT OSS 120B</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>120</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1:56</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>DeepSeek V3.2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>671</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>3:22</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 53.8906px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span>GLM 5 (FP8)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>745</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>5:43</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Kimi-K2.5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>1000</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>4:26</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><h2><span>Limitations</span></h2><p><span>An important downside of vLLM-Lens is that it provides a relatively small subset of all possible top-down interpretability techniques, currently focussing exclusively on interaction with the residual stream. We’ll extend features as we find more use cases, and we’ve found coding agents can also relatively easily add additional hooks, so if you’re working with large models and/or need faster inference and feedback cycles, it may well be useful for you. By contrast for other use cases you may find </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/"><span>nnsight</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://github.com/TransformerLensOrg/TransformerLens"><span>TransformerLens</span></a><span> to be a better fit.</span></p><h1><span>Technical Approach</span></h1><p><span>The vLLM plugin system isn’t well documented and we found that coding agents struggle to reason about vLLM internals, so we provide a brief overview of the technical approach here. vLLM-Lens registers as a </span><a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/stable/design/plugin_system"><span>vLLM plugin</span></a><span> and injects itself into vLLM's processing pipeline in 3 locations:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Intercepting generate calls.</span></b><span> To utilise the plugin, you can pass </span><a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/stable/api/vllm/sampling_params/#vllm.sampling_params.SamplingParams.extra_args"><span>extra args</span></a><span> such as output_residual_stream or apply_steering_vectors in the sampling parameters. The plugin extracts these, initialises relevant </span><a href="https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.Tensor.register_hook.html"><span>PyTorch hooks</span></a><span> if they're not already set up (by adding a </span><a href="https://docs.vllm.ai/en/stable/cli/run-batch/?h=worker+extension#-worker-extension-cls"><span>worker extension</span></a><span>) and sends steering vectors directly to workers (vLLM typically has one worker per GPU).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Per-sample hook operations.</span></b><span> vLLM dynamically batches tokens from multiple concurrent requests into a single forward pass, so a core challenge is "book-keeping" - working out which operations (e.g., activation extraction) should be applied to which parts of the forward pass. To do this we read the forward_context metadata, utilising the query_start_loc (a tensor of token boundaries per request) and req_ids (mapping batch index to request ID). We then, for example, apply steering to just the slices that correspond to the sample that requested it. Any extracted activations are moved to CPU RAM and compressed (lossless), ready to be requested by the vLLM scheduler process once generation for that sample has completed. Steering runs on all tensor-parallel ranks (since it modifies the forward pass), but capture operations only run on TP rank 0 (the residual streams are identical across TP replicas after all-reduce).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Response collation.</span></b><span> The plugin intercepts the response before it is sent to the client, at which point it queries the relevant vLLM processes for any requested activations. It trims surplus activations, as vLLM does under the hook with tokens (the scheduler often gets ahead of the number of tokens it needs to generate, before stopping). Activations are then returned to the client.</span></li></ol><h1><span>Credits</span></h1><p><span>Thanks to Satvik Golechha for the original idea of doing this with vLLM, and the </span><a href="https://nnsight.net/documentation/tracing/"><span>nnsight</span></a><span> team for inspiration. Thanks to Walter Laurito and Geoffrey Irving for valuable feedback.</span></p><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaz2eukp31mu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaz2eukp31mu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/situationalawareness.pdf"><span>Defined</span></a><span> as attempting to locate or alter information in a model without full understanding of how it is processed.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7ithb3qhkl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7ithb3qhkl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In practice it’s more typical to run probes on a subset of generated tokens, but the scorer here runs on all tokens for simplicity.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngq1um1x5yee"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgq1um1x5yee">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> At the time of writing, it supports vLLM 15.1 only.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnonqljeynxz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefonqljeynxz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>vLLM automatically determines an appropriate dynamic batch size during execution (a behaviour inherited by vLLM-Lens). For the Hugging Face Transformers, nnsight (transformers version) and TransformerLens libraries, we instead perform a simple search procedure: beginning at a batch size of 512 and iteratively halving until the run completes without GPU out-of-memory errors, after which we report the runtime of the largest successful configuration. For nnsight (vLLM backend), dynamic batching follows vLLM’s default behaviour and does not trigger GPU memory issues; however, CPU memory limits can still be encountered, which we resolved by manually calculating the most efficient batch size.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7pnpadn0f36"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7pnpadn0f36">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We think this was likely mostly due to the issues addressed by </span><a href="https://github.com/ndif-team/nnsight/pull/652"><span>https://github.com/ndif-team/nnsight/pull/652</span></a><span> , and that we had to enable batching to avoid out-of-memory issues as a result of these issues. A provisional experiment with the version of nnsight from that PR found performance to be the same as vLLM-Lens with a single-GPU test, but nnsight was 1.9x slower with a 4-GPU test (TP=4).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3bs27nZQuEcKhXf7q/vllm-lens-fast-interpretability-tooling-that-scales-to#comments">Discuss</a>
