## Engineering

### [Fragments: April 21](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-21.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Last week Thoughtworks released the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">34th volume of our Technology Radar</a>. This radar is our biannual survey of our experience of the technology scene, highlighting tools, techniques, platforms, and languages that we’ve used or otherwise caught our eye. This edition contains 118 blips, each briefly describing our impressions of one of these elements.</p>

<p>As we would expect, the radar is dominated by AI-oriented topics. Part of this is revisiting familiar ground with LLM-assisted eyes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>An interesting consequence of AI in software development is that it’s not only forcing us to look to the future; it’s also pushing us to revisit the foundations of our craft. While assembling this edition, we found ourselves returning to many established techniques, from pair programming to zero trust architecture, and from mutation testing to DORA metrics. We also revisited core principles of software craftsmanship, such as clean code, deliberate design, testability and accessibility as a first-class concern. This is not nostalgia, but a necessary counterweight to the speed at which AI tools can generate complexity. We also observed a resurgence of the command line: After years of abstracting it away in the name of usability, agentic tools are bringing developers back to the terminal as a primary interface.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was especially happy to see my colleague Jim Gumbley added to the writing team, he’s been a regular source of security information for me over the years, including working on this site’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/agile-threat-modelling.html">Threat Modeling Guide</a>. Having a strong security presence on the radar team is especially important given the serious security concerns around using LLMs. One of the themes of the radar is securing “permission hungry” agents:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>“Permission hungry” describes the bind at the heart of the current agent moment: the agents worth building are the ones that need access to everything. OpenClaw and Claude Cowork supervise real work tasks; Gas Town coordinates agent swarms across entire codebases. These agents require broad access to private data, external communication and real systems — each arguing that the payoff justifies it.</p>

  <p>However, like a skier who’s just learned to turn and confidently points themselves at the hardest black run, the safeguards haven’t caught up with that ambition. The appetite for access collides with unsolved problems. Prompt injection means models still can’t reliably distinguish trusted instructions from untrusted input.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Given all of this, many of this radar’s blips are about Harness Engineering, indeed the radar meeting was a major source of ideas for <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Birgitta’s excellent article</a> on the subject. The radar includes several blips suggesting the guides and sensors necessary for a well-fitting harness. I expect that when the next radar appears in six months time, that list will increase.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Mike Mason looks <a href="https://mikemason.ca/writing/ai-slop-code-april-2026/">what happens when developers aren’t reading the code</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The Python codebase Claude produced was largely working. Unit tests passed, and a few hours of real-world testing showed it was successfully managing a fairly complex piece of my infrastructure. But somewhere around 100KB of total code I noticed something: the main file had grown to about 50KB (2,000 lines) and Claude Code, when it needed to make edits, had started reaching for sed to find and modify code within that file. When I saw that, it was a serious alarm bell.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As well as the experience of “a friend”, he ponders the 500,000 lines of Claude Code after the leak.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Both things are true: there is good architecture in Claude Code, and there is also an incomprehensible mess. That’s actually the point. You don’t get to know which is which without reading the code.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His conclusion is a rough framework. Throw-away analysis scripts are fine to vibe away. Tooling you need to maintain and durable code, needs regular human review - even if it’s just a human asking a model to evaluate the code with some hints as to what good code looks like</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The moment you say “I’m getting uncomfortable with how big this is getting, can we do something better?” it does the right thing: sensible decomposition, new classes, sometimes even unit tests for the new thing. It knew, it just didn’t volunteer it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He does recommend being serious with <code>CLAUDE.md</code>, I don’t know if he’s tried many of the patterns that Rahul Garg has recently posted to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/">break the similar frustration loop</a> that he saw.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davies poses an <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/authentic-is-as-authentic-does-or">annoying philosophy thought experiment</a> for us to consider how we feel about LLMs indulging in ghost writing.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DOGE dismantled many useful things during their brief period with the wood chipper. One of these was DirectFile, a government program that supported people filing their taxes online. Don Moynihan has talked to many folks involved in Direct File, <a href="https://donmoynihan.substack.com/p/what-the-death-of-direct-file-tells">has penned a worthwhile essay</a> that isn’t just relevant to DirectFile and other U.S. government technology projects, but indeed any technology initiative in a large organization.</p>

<p>Moynihan highlights:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>a paradox of government reform: the simpler a potential change appears, the more likely that it has not been implemented because it features deceptive complexity that others have tried and failed to resolve.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I’ve heard that tale in many a large corporation too</p>

<p>One way government initiatives are different is that, at its best, it’s built on an attitude of public service</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Many who worked on Direct File drew a sharp contrast with DOGE and their approach to building tech products. One point of distinction was DOGE’s seeming disinterest in public interest goals and of the public itself: “if you do not think government has a responsibility to serve people, I think it draws into question how good are you going to be at making government work better for people if you just don’t believe in that underlying principle”</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The tragedy for U.S. taxpayers like me is that we’ve lost an effective way to go through the annual hassle of taxes. In addition the IRS is much weaker - it’s lost 25% of its staff and its budget is 40% below what it was in 2010. Much though we hate tax collectors, this isn’t a good thing. An efficient tax system is an important part of national security, many historians consider the ability to raise taxes effectively was an important reason why Britain won its century-long struggle with France in the Eighteenth century. A wonky tax system is also a major reason why the French monarchy, so powerful at the start of that century, fell to revolution. Indeed there is considerable evidence that increasing the budget of the IRS <a href="https://budgetlab.yale.edu/research/revenue-and-distributional-effects-irs-funding#:~:text=Behavioral%20estimates%20are%20always%20uncertain,from%20taxpayers%20who%20are%20audited.">would more than pay for itself by increasing revenue</a>.</p>

---

### [Fragments: April 14](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-14.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I attended the first Pragmatic Summit early this year, and while there host
<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CZs8J1ZD0CE">Gergely Orosz interviewed Kent Beck and myself on stage</a>. The video runs for about half-an-hour.</p>

<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CZs8J1ZD0CE"><img alt="A photo of Gergely, Kent, and myself on the stage. We are all bedazzled by the vibrancy of Kent's trousers" src="https://martinfowler.com/img/catalog/2026-prag-summit.png" /></a></p>

<p>I always enjoy nattering with Kent like this, and Gergely pushed into some worthwhile topics. Given
the timing, AI dominated the conversation - we compared it to earlier
technology shifts, the experience of agile methods, the role of TDD, the
danger of unhealthy performance metrics, and how to thrive in an AI-native
industry.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Perl is a language I used a little, but never loved. However the definitive book on it, by its designer Larry Wall, contains a wonderful gem. The three virtues of a programmer: hubris, impatience - and above all - <em>laziness</em>.</p>

<p>Bryan Cantrill <a href="https://bcantrill.dtrace.org/2026/04/12/the-peril-of-laziness-lost/">also loves this virtue</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Of these virtues, I have always found laziness to be the most profound: packed within its tongue-in-cheek self-deprecation is a commentary on not just the need for abstraction, but the aesthetics of it. Laziness drives us to make the system as simple as possible (but no simpler!) — to develop the powerful abstractions that then allow us to do much more, much more easily.</p>

  <p>Of course, the implicit wink here is that it takes a lot of work to be lazy</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Understanding how to think about a problem domain by building abstractions (models) is my favorite part of programming. I love it because I think it’s what gives me a deeper understanding of a problem domain, and because once I find a good set of abstractions, I get a buzz from the way they make difficulties melt away, allowing me to achieve much more functionality with less lines of code.</p>

<p>Cantrill worries that AI is so good at writing code, we risk losing that virtue, something that’s reinforced by brogrammers bragging about how they produce thirty-seven thousand lines of code a day.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The problem is that LLMs inherently lack the virtue of laziness. Work costs nothing to an LLM. LLMs do not feel a need to optimize for their own (or anyone’s) future time, and will happily dump more and more onto a layercake of garbage. Left unchecked, LLMs will make systems larger, not better — appealing to perverse vanity metrics, perhaps, but at the cost of everything that matters. As such, LLMs highlight how essential our human laziness is: our finite time forces us to develop crisp abstractions in part because we don’t want to waste our (human!) time on the consequences of clunky ones. The best engineering is always borne of constraints, and the constraint of our time places limits on the cognitive load of the system that we’re willing to accept. This is what drives us to make the system simpler, despite its essential complexity.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This reflection particularly struck me this Sunday evening. I’d spent a bit of time making a modification of how my music playlist generator worked. I needed a new capability, spent some time adding it, got frustrated at how long it was taking, and wondered about maybe throwing a coding agent at it. More thought led to realizing that I was doing it in a more complicated way than it needed to be. I was including a facility that I didn’t need, and <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/Yagni.html">by applying yagni</a>, I could make the whole thing much easier, doing the task in just a couple of dozen lines of code.</p>

<p>If I had used an LLM for this, it may well have done the task much more quickly, but would it have made a similar over-complication? If so would I just shrug and say LGTM? Would that complication cause me (or the LLM) problems in the future?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jessica Kerr (Jessitron) has a simple example of <a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/04/06/adding-correctness-conditions-to-code-changes/">applying the principle of Test-Driven Development to prompting agents</a>. She wants all updates to include updating the documentation.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Instructions – We can change AGENTS.md to instruct our coding agent to look for documentation files and update them.</p>

  <p>Verification – We can add a reviewer agent to check each PR for missed documentation updates.</p>

  <p>This is two changes, so I can break this work into two parts. Which of these should we do first?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Of course my initial comment about TDD answers that question</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Mark Little prodded an old memory of mine as he wondered about to work with AIs that are over-confident of their knowledge and thus prone to make up answers to questions, or to act when they should be more hesitant. He draws inspiration from an old, low-budget, but classic SciFi movie: <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_Star_(film)">Dark Star</a>. I saw that movie once in my 20s (ie a long time ago), but I still remember the crisis scene where a crew member has to use <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=S-xUjmJkO8g">philosophical argument to prevent a sentient bomb from detonating</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Doolittle: You have no absolute proof that Sergeant Pinback ordered you to detonate.<br />
Bomb #20:  I recall distinctly the detonation order. My memory is good on matters like these.<br />
Doolittle: Of course you remember it, but all you remember is merely a series of sensory impulses which you now realize have no real, definite connection with outside reality.<br />
Bomb #20:  True. But since this is so, I have no real proof that you’re telling me all this.<br />
Doolittle: That’s all beside the point. I mean, the concept is valid no matter where it originates.<br />
Bomb #20:  Hmmmm….<br />
Doolittle: So, if you detonate…<br />
Bomb #20:  In nine seconds….<br />
Doolittle: …you could be doing so on the basis of false data.<br />
Bomb #20:  I have no proof it was false data.<br />
Doolittle: You have no proof it was correct data!<br />
Bomb #20:  I must think on this further.<br /></p>
</blockquote>

<p>Doolittle has to expand the bomb’s consciousness, teaching it to doubt its sensors. <a href="https://markclittle.blogspot.com/2026/03/dark-star-and-ai-morality.html">As Little puts it:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>That’s a useful metaphor for where we are with AI today. Most AI systems are optimised for decisiveness. Given an input, produce an output. Given ambiguity, resolve it probabilistically. Given uncertainty, infer. This works well in bounded domains, but it breaks down in open systems where the cost of a wrong decision is asymmetric or irreversible. In those cases, the correct behaviour is often deferral, or even deliberate inaction. But inaction is not a natural outcome of most AI architectures. It has to be designed in.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>In my more human interactions, I’ve always valued doubt, and distrust people who operate under undue certainty. Doubt doesn’t necessarily lead to indecisiveness, but it does suggest that we include the risk of inaccurate information or faulty reasoning into decisions with profound consequences.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If we want AI systems that can operate safely without constant human oversight, we need to teach them not just how to decide, but when not to. In a world of increasing autonomy, restraint isn’t a limitation, it’s a capability. And in many cases, it may be the most important one we build.</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [Alan Turing play in Cambridge MA](https://martinfowler.com/articles/202604-turing.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Last night I saw Central Square Theater’s excellent production of <a href="https://www.centralsquaretheater.org/shows/breaking-the-code-2026/">Breaking the Code</a>. It’s about Alan Turing, who made a monumental contribution to both my profession and the fate of free democracies. Well worth seeing if you’re in the Boston area this month.</p>

### [Fragments: April  9](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-09.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I mostly link to written material here, but I’ve recently listened to two excellent podcasts that I can recommend.</p>

<p>Anyone who regularly reads these fragments knows that I’m a big fan of Simon Willison, his (also very fragmentary) posts have earned a regular spot in my RSS reader. But the problem with fragments, however valuable, is that they don’t provide a cohesive overview of the situation. So <a href="https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/an-ai-state-of-the-union">his podcast with Lenny Rachitsky</a> is a welcome survey of that state of world as seen through a discerning pair of eyeballs. He paints a good picture of how programming has changed for him since the “November inflection point”, important patterns for this work, and his concern about the security bomb nestled inside the beast.</p>

<p>My other great listening was on a regular podcast that I listen to, as <a href="https://newsletter.pragmaticengineer.com/p/scaling-uber-with-thuan-pham-ubers">Gergely Orosz interviewed Thuan Pham</a> - the former CTO of Uber. As with so many of Gergely’s podcasts, they focused on Thuan Pham’s fascinating career direction, giving listeners an opportunity to learn from a successful professional. There’s also an informative insight into Uber’s use of microservices (they had 5000 of them), and the way high-growth software necessarily gets rewritten a lot (a phenomenon I dubbed <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SacrificialArchitecture.html">Sacrificial Architecture</a>)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Axios published their <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/3/supply-chain-social-engineering/">post-mortem on their recent supply chain compromise</a>. It’s quite a story, the attackers spent a couple of weeks developing contact with the lead maintainer, leading to a video call where the meeting software indicated something on the maintainer’s system was out of date. That led to the maintainer installing the update, which in fact was a Remote Access Trojan (RAT).</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>they tailored this process specifically to me by doing the following:</p>

  <ul>
    <li>they reached out masquerading as the founder of a company they had cloned the companys founders likeness as well as the company itself.</li>
    <li>they then invited me to a real slack workspace. this workspace was branded to the companies ci and named in a plausible manner. the slack was thought out very well, they had channels where they were sharing linked-in posts, the linked in posts i presume just went to the real companys account but it was super convincing etc. they even had what i presume were fake profiles of the team of the company but also number of other oss maintainers.</li>
    <li>they scheduled a meeting with me to connect. the meeting was on ms teams. the meeting had what seemed to be a group of people that were involved.</li>
    <li>the meeting said something on my system was out of date. i installed the missing item as i presumed it was something to do with teams, and this was the RAT.</li>
    <li>everything was extremely well co-ordinated looked legit and was done in a professional manner.</li>
  </ul>
</blockquote>

<p>Simon Willison has a <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Apr/3/supply-chain-social-engineering/">summary and further links</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I recently bumped into <a href="https://diataxis.fr/how-to-use-diataxis/">Diátaxis</a>, a framework for organizing technical documentation. I only looked at it briefly, but there’s much to like. In particular I appreciated how it classified four forms of documentation:</p>

<ul>
  <li>Tutorials: to learn how to use the product</li>
  <li>How-to guides: for users to follow to achieve particular goals with the product</li>
  <li>Reference: to describe what the product does</li>
  <li>Explanations: background and context to educate the user on the product’s rationale</li>
</ul>

<p>The distinction between tutorials and how-to guides is interesting</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A tutorial serves the needs of the user who is at study. Its obligation is to provide a successful learning experience. A how-to guide serves the needs of the user who is at work. Its obligation is to help the user accomplish a task.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I also appreciated its point of pulling explanations out into separate areas. The idea is that other forms should contain only minimal explanations, linking to the explanation material for more depth. That way we keep the flow on the goal and allow the user to seek deeper explanations in their own way. The study/work distinction between explanation and reference mirrors that same distinction between tutorials and how-to guides.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>For eight years, Lalit Maganti wanted a set of tools for working with SQLite. But it would be hard and tedious work, “getting into the weeds of SQLite source code, a fiendishly difficult codebase to understand”. So he didn’t try it. But after the <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Jan/4/inflection/">November inflection point</a>, he decided to tackle this need.</p>

<p><a href="https://lalitm.com/post/building-syntaqlite-ai/">His account of this exercise</a> is an excellent description of the benefits and perils of developing with AI agents.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Through most of January, I iterated, acting as semi-technical manager and delegating almost all the design and all the implementation to Claude. Functionally, I ended up in a reasonable place: a parser in C extracted from SQLite sources using a bunch of Python scripts, a formatter built on top, support for both the SQLite language and the PerfettoSQL extensions, all exposed in a web playground.</p>

  <p>But when I reviewed the codebase in detail in late January, the downside was obvious: the codebase was complete spaghetti. I didn’t understand large parts of the Python source extraction pipeline, functions were scattered in random files without a clear shape, and a few files had grown to several thousand lines. It was extremely fragile; it solved the immediate problem but it was never going to cope with my larger vision, never mind integrating it into the Perfetto tools. The saving grace was that it had proved the approach was viable and generated more than 500 tests, many of which I felt I could reuse.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He threw it all away and worked more closely with the AI on the second attempt, with lots of thinking about the design, reviewing all the code, and refactoring with every step</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In the rewrite, refactoring became the core of my workflow. After every large batch of generated code, I’d step back and ask “is this ugly?” Sometimes AI could clean it up. Other times there was a large-scale abstraction that AI couldn’t see but I could; I’d give it the direction and let it execute. If you have taste, the cost of a wrong approach drops dramatically because you can restructure quickly.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He ended up with a working system, and the AI proved its value in allowing him to tackle something that he’d been leaving on the todo pile for years. But even with the rewrite, the AI had its potholes.</p>

<p>His conclusion of the relative value of AI in different scenarios:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>When I was working on something I already understood deeply, AI was excellent….
When I was working on something I could describe but didn’t yet know, AI was good but required more care….
When I was working on something where I didn’t even know what I wanted, AI was somewhere between unhelpful and harmful…</p>
</blockquote>

<p>At the heart of this is that AI works at its best when there is an objectively checkable answer. If we want an implementation that can pass some tests, then AI does a good job. But when it came to the public API:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I spent several days in early March doing nothing but API refactoring, manually fixing things any experienced engineer would have instinctively avoided but AI made a total mess of. There’s no test or objective metric for “is this API pleasant to use” and “will this API help users solve the problems they have” and that’s exactly why the coding agents did so badly at it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I became familiar with Ryan Avent’s writing when he wrote the Free Exchange column for The Economist. <a href="https://ryanavent.substack.com/p/faith-and-the-future">His recent post</a> talks about how James Talarico and Zohran Mamdani have made their religion an important part of their electoral appeal, and their faith is centered on caring for others. He explains that a focus on care leads to an important perspective on economic growth.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The first thing to understand is that we should not want growth for its own sake. What is good about growth is that it expands our collective capacities: we come to know more and we are able to do more. This, in turn, allows us to alleviate suffering, to discover more things about the universe, and to spend more time being complete people.</p>
</blockquote>

      proposes a <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/feedback-flywheel.html">structured feedback practice</a> that harvests learnings from AI
      sessions and feeds them back into the team's shared artifacts, turning
      individual experience into collective improvement.</p>

### [Principles of Mechanical Sympathy](https://martinfowler.com/articles/mechanical-sympathy-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/mechanical-sympathy-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/mechanical-sympathy-principles/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Modern hardware is remarkably fast, but software often fails to
      leverage it. <b class="author">Caer Sanders</b> has found it valuable to guide
      their work with <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/mechanical-sympathy-principles.html">mechanical sympathy</a> - the practice of creating
      software that is sympathetic to its underlying hardware. They distill this
      practice into everyday principles: predictable memory access, awareness of
      cache lines, single-writer, and natural batching.</p>

### [Fragments: April  2](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-04-02.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>As we see LLMs churn out scads of code, folks have increasingly turned to Cognitive Debt as a metaphor for capturing how a team can lose understanding of what a system does. Margaret-Anne Storey thinks a good way of thinking about these problems is to consider <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22106">three layers of system health</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>Technical debt lives in code. It accumulates when implementation decisions compromise future changeability. It limits how systems can change.</li>
    <li>Cognitive debt lives in people. It accumulates when shared understanding of the system erodes faster than it is replenished. It limits how teams can reason about change.</li>
    <li>Intent debt lives in artifacts. It accumulates when the goals and constraints that should guide the system are poorly captured or maintained. It limits whether the system continues to reflect what we meant to build and it limits how humans and AI agents can continue to evolve the system effectively.</li>
  </ul>
</blockquote>

<p>While I’m getting a bit bemused by debt metaphor proliferation, this way of thinking does make a fair bit of sense. The article includes useful sections to diagnose and mitigate each kind of debt. The three interact with each other, and the article outlines some general activities teams should do to keep it all under control</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In the article she references a recent paper by Shaw and Nave at the Wharton School that <a href="https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6097646">adds LLMs to Kahneman’s two-system model of thinking</a>.</p>

<p>Kahneman’s book, “Thinking Fast and Slow”, is one of my favorite books. Its central idea is that humans have two systems of cognition. System 1 (intuition) makes rapid decisions, often barely-consciously. System 2 (deliberation) is when we apply deliberate thinking to a problem. He observed that to save energy we default to intuition, and that sometimes gets us into trouble when we  overlook things that we would have spotted had we applied deliberation to the problem.</p>

<p>Shaw and Nave consider AI as System 3</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A consequence of System 3 is the introduction of cognitive surrender, characterized by uncritical reliance on externally generated artificial reasoning, bypassing System 2. Crucially, we distinguish cognitive surrender, marked by passive trust and uncritical evaluation of external information, from cognitive offloading, which involves strategic delegation of cognition during deliberation.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>It’s a long paper, that goes into detail on this “Tri-System theory of cognition” and reports on several experiments they’ve done to test how well this theory can predict behavior (at least within a lab).</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve seen a few illustrations recently that use the symbols “&lt; &gt;” as part of an icon to illustrate code. That strikes me as rather odd, I can’t think of any programming language that uses “&lt; &gt;” to surround program elements. Why that and not, say, “{ }”?</p>

<p>Obviously the reason is that they are thinking of HTML (or maybe XML), which is even more obvious when they use “&lt;/&gt;” in their icons. But programmers don’t <em>program</em> in HTML.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Ajey Gore thinks about <a href="https://ajeygore.in/content/the-expensive-thing">if coding agents make coding free, what becomes the expensive thing</a>? His answer is verification.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>What does “correct” mean for an ETA algorithm in Jakarta traffic versus Ho Chi Minh City? What does a “successful” driver allocation look like when you’re balancing earnings fairness, customer wait time, and fleet utilisation simultaneously? When hundreds of engineers are shipping into ~900 microservices around the clock, “correct” isn’t one definition — it’s thousands of definitions, all shifting, all context-dependent. These aren’t edge cases. They’re the entire job.</p>

  <p>And they’re precisely the kind of judgment that agents cannot perform for you.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Increasingly I’m seeing a view that agents do really well when they have good, preferably automated, verification for their work. This encourages such things as <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/TestDrivenDevelopment.html">Test Driven Development</a>. That’s still a lot of verification to do, which suggests we should see more effort to find ways to make it easier for humans to comprehend larger ranges of tests.</p>

<p>While I agree with most of what Ajey writes here, I do have a quibble with his view of legacy migration. He thinks it’s a delusion that “agentic coding will finally crack legacy modernisation”. I agree with him that agentic <em>coding</em> is overrated in a legacy context, but I have seen compelling evidence that LLMs help a great deal in <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/legacy-modernization-gen-ai.html">understanding what legacy code is doing</a>.</p>

<p>The big consequence of Ajey’s assessment is that we’ll need to reorganize around verification rather than writing code:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If agents handle execution, the human job becomes designing verification systems, defining quality, and handling the ambiguous cases agents can’t resolve. Your org chart should reflect this. Practically, this means your Monday morning standup changes. Instead of “what did we ship?” the question becomes “what did we validate?” Instead of tracking output, you’re tracking whether the output was right. The team that used to have ten engineers building features now has three engineers and seven people defining acceptance criteria, designing test harnesses, and monitoring outcomes. That’s the reorganisation. It’s uncomfortable because it demotes the act of building and promotes the act of judging. Most engineering cultures resist this. The ones that don’t will win.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>One the questions comes up when we think of LLMs-as-programmers is whether there is a future for source code. David Cassel on The New Stack has an article summarizing <a href="https://thenewstack.io/ai-programming-languages-future/">several views of the future of code</a>. Some folks are experimenting with entirely new languages built with the LLM in mind, others think that existing languages, especially strictly typed languages like TypeScript and Rust will be the best fit for LLMs. It’s an overview article, one that has lots of quotations, but not much analysis in itself - but it’s worth a read as a good overview of the discussion.</p>

<p>I’m interested to see how all this will play out. I do think there’s still a role for humans to work with LLMs to build useful abstractions in which to talk about what the code does - essentially the DDD notion of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/UbiquitousLanguage.html">Ubiquitous Language</a>. Last year Unmesh and I talked about <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/convo-llm-abstractions.html">growing a language</a> with LLMs. As Unmesh put it</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Programming isn’t just typing coding syntax that computers can understand and execute; it’s shaping a solution. We slice the problem into focused pieces, bind related data and behaviour together, and—crucially—choose names that expose intent. Good names cut through complexity and turn code into a schematic everyone can follow. The most creative act is this continual weaving of names that reveal the structure of the solution that maps clearly to the problem we are trying to solve.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Encoding Team Standards](https://martinfowler.com/articles/reduce-friction-ai/encoding-team-standards.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>AI coding assistants respond to whoever is prompting, and the quality
      of what they produce depends on how well the prompter articulates team
      standards. <b class="author">Rahul Garg</b> proposes treating the instructions
      that govern AI interactions (generation, refactoring, security, review) as
      infrastructure: versioned, reviewed, and shared artifacts that encode
      tacit team knowledge into executable instructions, making quality
      consistent regardless of who is at the keyboard.</p>

### [Fragments: March 26](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-03-26.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Anthropic carried a study, done by getting its model to <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/features/81k-interviews">interview some 80,000 users</a> to understand their opinions about AI, what they hope from it, and what they fear. Two things stood out to me.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s easy to assume there are AI optimists and AI pessimists, divided into separate camps. But what we actually found were people organized around what they value—financial security, learning, human connection— watching advancing AI capabilities while managing both hope and fear at once.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>That makes sense, if asked whether I’m a an AI booster or an AI doomer, I answer “yes”. I am both fascinated by its impact on my profession, expectant of the benefits it will bring to our world, and worried by the harms that will come from it. Powerful technologies rarely yield simple consequences.</p>

<p>The other thing that struck me was that, despite most people mixing the two,  there was an overall variance between optimism and pessimism with AI by geography. In general, the less developed the country, the more optimism about AI.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Julias Shaw describes how to fix a <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/youre-wasting-effort-you-put-spec-driven-development-julias-shaw-dxmcc/">gap in many people’s use of specs</a> to drive LLMs:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Here’s what I keep seeing: the specification-driven development (SDD) conversation has exploded. The internet is overflowing with people saying you should write a spec before prompting. Describe the behavior you want. Define the constraints. Give the agent guardrails. Good advice. I often follow it myself.</p>

  <p>But almost nobody takes the next step. Encoding those specifications into automated tests that actually enforce the contract.</p>

  <p>And the strange part is, most developers outside the extreme programming crowd don’t realize they need to. They genuinely believe the spec document is the safety net. It isn’t. The spec document is the blueprint. The safety net is the test suite that catches the moment your code drifts away from it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As well as explaining why it’s important to have such a test suite, he provides an astute five-step checklist to turn spec documents into executable tests.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Lawfare has a long article on potential <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/iran-will-retaliate-in-the-us-we-may-not-see-it-in-time">problems countering covert action by Iran</a>. It’s a long article, and I confess I only skip-read it. It begins by outlining a bunch of plots hatched in the last few years. Then it says:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If these examples seem repetitive, it’s because they are. Iran has proved itself relentless in its efforts to carry out attacks on U.S. soil—and the U.S., for its part, has demonstrated that it is capable of countering those efforts. The above examples show how robustly the U.S. national security apparatus was able to respond, largely through the FBI and the Justice Department….</p>

  <p>That is, potentially, until now. The current administration has decimated the national security elements of both agencies through firings and forced resignations. People with decades of experience in building interagency and critical source relationships around the world, handling high-pressure, complicated investigations straddling classified and unclassified spaces, and acting in time to prevent violence and preserve evidence have been pushed out the door. Those who remain not only have to stretch to make up for the personnel deficit but also are being pulled away by White House priorities not tied to the increasing threat of an Iranian response.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The article goes into detail about these cuts, and the threats that may exploit the resulting gaps.</p>

<p>It’s the nature of national security people to highlight potential threats and call for more resources and power. But it’s also the nature of enemies to find weak spots and look to cause havoc. I wonder what we’ll think should we read this article again in a few years time</p>

---

## LLM Infrastructure

### [v0.20.0](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.20.0)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <h1>vLLM v0.20.0</h1>
<h2>Highlights</h2>
<p>This release features 546 commits from 257 contributors (83 new)!</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>CUDA 13.0 default</strong>: Default CUDA wheel switched to CUDA 13.0; architecture lists and build-args cleaned up (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39878">#39878</a>). As a general rule of thumb, our CUDA version policy follows PyTorch's.</li>
<li><strong>PyTorch 2.11 upgrade</strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34644">#34644</a>): vLLM ships on torch 2.11 for CUDA; XPU temporarily remains on torch-xpu 2.10 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39656">#39656</a>). This is a breaking change for environment dependency.</li>
<li><strong>Transformers v5</strong>: vLLM now runs on HuggingFace <code>transformers&gt;=5</code> (landed in v0.19.1); v0.20.0 carries continued v4/v5 compat fixes including PaddleOCR-VL image processor <code>max_pixels</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38629">#38629</a>) and Mistral YaRN warning (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37292">#37292</a>).</li>
<li><strong>FlashAttention 4 as default MLA prefill</strong>: FA4 re-enabled as the default MLA prefill backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38819">#38819</a>) with head-dim 512 and paged-KV support on SM90+ (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38835">#38835</a>), plus an upstream FA4 sync (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38690">#38690</a>).</li>
<li><strong>TurboQuant 2-bit KV cache</strong>: New attention backend delivering 2-bit KV cache compression with 4× capacity (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38479">#38479</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Online quantization frontend</strong>: New end-to-end online quantization frontend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38138">#38138</a>), with docs (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39736">#39736</a>); experts_int8 consolidated into the FP8 online path (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38463">#38463</a>).</li>
<li><strong>vLLM IR</strong>: Initial IR skeleton with rms_norm op (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33825">#33825</a>), OOT-platform kernel imports (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38807">#38807</a>), and gemma_rms_norm reworked on IR (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39014">#39014</a>) — foundation for future kernel work.</li>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2 advances</strong>: Eagle prefill full-CUDA-graph (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37588">#37588</a>), auto-resolve cudagraph mode/sizes from attention backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32936">#32936</a>), fused probabilistic rejection sample kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38496">#38496</a>), config validation for unsupported features (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38758">#38758</a>), and piecewise-fallback disabled for eagle draft decodes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39773">#39773</a>).</li>
<li><strong>MoE refactor series</strong>: Unquantized migrated to Full Oracle Flow (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36286">#36286</a>), SharedExperts class (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35153">#35153</a>), DefaultMoERunner split (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35326">#35326</a>), ZeroExpertFusedMoE in new framework (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35549">#35549</a>), compressed_tensors_moe.py split up (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38960">#38960</a>), and MoE DP chunking removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39107">#39107</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Model Support</h3>
<ul>
<li>New architectures: EXAONE-4.5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39388">#39388</a>), BharatGen Param2MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38000">#38000</a>), Phi-4-reasoning-vision-15B (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38306">#38306</a>), Cheers multimodal (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38788">#38788</a>), telechat3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38510">#38510</a>), FireRedLID (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39290">#39290</a>), jina-reranker-v3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38800">#38800</a>), Jina Embeddings v5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39575">#39575</a>), Nemotron-v3 VL Nano/Super (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39747">#39747</a>).</li>
<li>Quantization formats: GGUF support for MiniMax-M2.1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36965">#36965</a>), non-standard GGUF quant types with prefix such as UD-IQ1_S (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39471">#39471</a>).</li>
<li>Speculative decoding: Eagle3 for MiniMax-M2 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37512">#37512</a>).</li>
<li>LoRA: Qwen3ASRForConditionalGeneration (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37247">#37247</a>), Gemma4ForConditionalGeneration (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39291">#39291</a>), dual-CUDA-streams linear layer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35721">#35721</a>).</li>
<li>Multimodal MRoPE refresh: mm_features-based MRoPE for Ernie-4.5 VL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39753">#39753</a>), Keye-VL / Keye-1.5-VL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39869">#39869</a>), PaddleOCR-VL (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39888">#39888</a>).</li>
<li>Parakeet UX / perf enhancements (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39423">#39423</a>); ColModernVBERT updated for latest HF checkpoint (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39307">#39307</a>); NemotronH default <code>mamba_ssm_cache_dtype=float32</code> with NemotronHNanoVLV2 auto-hook (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39032">#39032</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Engine Core</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Model Runner V2</strong>: Full CUDA graph for eagle prefill (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37588">#37588</a>), auto cudagraph mode/sizes based on attention backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32936">#32936</a>), fused probabilistic rejection-sample kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38496">#38496</a>), config validation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38758">#38758</a>), eagle-draft piecewise fallback disabled (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39773">#39773</a>).</li>
<li><strong>vLLM IR</strong>: IR skeleton + rms_norm (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33825">#33825</a>), OOT kernel import hooks (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38807">#38807</a>), gemma_rms_norm on IR (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39014">#39014</a>).</li>
<li><strong>torch.compile</strong>: Opaque Objects on torch 2.11 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39286">#39286</a>), AOT compile with batch-invariance mode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39201">#39201</a>), Inductor cache nested under AOT dir (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39718">#39718</a>), split FX graph via codegen (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38657">#38657</a>), Inductor pre-grad passes re-enabled for torch≥2.12 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38944">#38944</a>), strings in custom ops without compile regressions (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38123">#38123</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Attention</strong>: FA4 as default MLA prefill (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38819">#38819</a>), head-dim 512 + paged-KV on sm90+FA4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38835">#38835</a>), FA4 upstream sync (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38690">#38690</a>), full CUDA graph for FlexAttention (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36298">#36298</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Helion kernels</strong>: torch.compile support for Helion kernels (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38592">#38592</a>).</li>
<li><strong>HMA / KV offload</strong>: GPU-side KV events for HMA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37688">#37688</a>), group block hashes/IDs tracked (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37109">#37109</a>), unified memory layout for offloading workers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37206">#37206</a>), <code>shutdown()</code> on OffloadingConnector (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39182">#39182</a>), request context passed through KV offload (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39185">#39185</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Features</strong>: NUMA binding for GPU workers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38635">#38635</a>), opt-in <code>VLLM_MEDIA_CACHE</code> media URL caching (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37123">#37123</a>), safe request abort when FSM fails to advance (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38663">#38663</a>), KV connector prioritized over internal registry (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38301">#38301</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Pluggable layers</strong>: Applied to llm_head / vocab embedding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33465">#33465</a>) and MoE layers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33556">#33556</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Mamba</strong>: Stochastic rounding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35753">#35753</a>), different Conv state layouts (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37416">#37416</a>), FlashInfer <code>selective_state_update</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36162">#36162</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Metrics &amp; scheduling</strong>: Labeled waiting-breakdown (capacity/deferred) metric (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38435">#38435</a>), API server handshake simplified (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39364">#39364</a>), mm-scheduler <code>get_num_embed</code> overhead reduced (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40143">#40143</a>), <code>request_id</code> on <code>FinishedRequestStats</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39710">#39710</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Executor</strong>: RayExecutorV2 introduced (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36836">#36836</a>); unified engine process monitoring with Ray backend (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35862">#35862</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Hardware &amp; Performance</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>NVIDIA</strong>: swapAB support for SM120 CUTLASS blockwise FP8 GEMM (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38325">#38325</a>), MXFP4 W4A4 CUTLASS MoE for SM100 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37463">#37463</a>), TRTLLM GEN NVFP4 MoE with non-512-aligned hidden dims via weight padding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39510">#39510</a>), TRTLLM FP8 MoE with shuffled weights + BlockMajorK layout (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38993">#38993</a>), fused qknorm+rope kernel on SM9.0 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37376">#37376</a>), tuned fused_moe config for RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39183">#39183</a>), ViT full CUDA graph for Qwen3-VL video (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38061">#38061</a>), fused FP8 output quantization into <code>merge_attn_states</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36518">#36518</a>), batched KV-cache swap via <code>cuMemcpyBatchAsync</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38460">#38460</a>).</li>
<li><strong>AMD ROCm</strong>: ZenCPU / AMD Zen CPU backend via zentorch (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39967">#39967</a>), RDNA 3.5/4 device IDs (gfx1150/1151/1201) (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38455">#38455</a>), MORI EP for unquantized MoE with AITER (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37529">#37529</a>), AITER gemm w8a8 ptpc integration (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33773">#33773</a>), TritonW4A16LinearKernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37352">#37352</a>), asymmetric INT8 in <code>TritonInt8ScaledMMLinearKernel</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38501">#38501</a>), <code>fused_silu_mul_block_quant</code> enabled (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38817">#38817</a>), KV-cache shuffle for <code>paged_attention_common</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32914">#32914</a>), MLA decode output zero-fill removed in AITER (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37539">#37539</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Intel XPU</strong>: Initial GDN attention for Qwen3-Next / Qwen3.5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33657">#33657</a>), XPU MXFP8 quant op (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38682">#38682</a>), XPU MXFP4 quant op (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39857">#39857</a>), per-channel FP8 linear (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38316">#38316</a>), FP8 KV cache on XPU (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37731">#37731</a>), <code>round_int8</code> for Intel Triton (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38825">#38825</a>).</li>
<li><strong>CPU</strong>: CPU draft-model speculative decoding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32662">#32662</a>), CPU int8 compute mode in AWQ (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35697">#35697</a>), head_size 512 in <code>cpu_attn</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38676">#38676</a>), gelu in <code>cpu_fused_moe</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38770">#38770</a>), OMP replacement (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36487">#36487</a>), BF16 GELU LUT on ARM (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37469">#37469</a>), W4A16 Autoround on CPU (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38192">#38192</a>), CPU affinity/memory mgmt refactor (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39781">#39781</a>), IBM Z s390x torch 2.11 builds (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39910">#39910</a>).</li>
<li><strong>DeepSeek / MLA / Indexer</strong>: Persistent TopK scheduler for DSV3.2 DSA decode (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37421">#37421</a>), DSV3.2 indexer fused weights projection (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38684">#38684</a>), Triton MLA perf fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33529">#33529</a>), indexer WK upcast to BF16 for fusion (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38928">#38928</a>), MLA indexer uniform-decode optimization for MTP&gt;1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39458">#39458</a>).</li>
<li><strong>GDN / Mamba</strong>: Kernel fusion in GDN (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37813">#37813</a>), TMA aligned with upstream FLA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38981">#38981</a>), GPU↔CPU syncs eliminated in prefill and spec-decode paths (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38361">#38361</a>, <a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38047">#38047</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Other</strong>: DeepGEMM integrated into the vLLM wheel via CMake (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37980">#37980</a>), Lustre FS checkpoint prefetching enabled by default (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39422">#39422</a>), Gemma4 fused routing Triton kernel (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39083">#39083</a>), Gemma4 embed_input_ids GPU/CPU sync removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39234">#39234</a>), Nemotron VL image/video preprocessing optimized (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40283">#40283</a>), SiLU block-quant fusion v1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32996">#32996</a>), bilinear_pos_embed Triton kernel for ViT (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37948">#37948</a>), mean-pooling optimization (~5.9% throughput) (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38559">#38559</a>), redundant-sync removal for pooling (~3.7% throughput) (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39113">#39113</a>), H2D pageable-memory copy reduction (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38794">#38794</a>), fused zero initializer for FP8 DeepGemm block-quant (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39547">#39547</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Large Scale Serving</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>EPLB</strong>: Alternative communication for EPLB weight exchange (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/33176">#33176</a>), mapping optimization with router record for prefill (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36261">#36261</a>).</li>
<li><strong>KV Offload / Connector</strong>: 3FS KVConnector (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37636">#37636</a>), unified memory layout for offloading workers (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37206">#37206</a>), cache_salt propagated through MP connector for per-user isolation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39837">#39837</a>), multi-connector metrics of same type (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40010">#40010</a>), LMCache block-allocation event (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38856">#38856</a>), LMCache MP save optimization with MLA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38810">#38810</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Disaggregated / NIXL / Mamba</strong>: Heterogeneous TP 3-read conv-state transfer for NIXL + Mamba (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37635">#37635</a>), Nixl bumped to 0.10.1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39922">#39922</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Quantization</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>New formats &amp; methods</strong>: TurboQuant 2-bit KV cache compression (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38479">#38479</a>), per-token-head INT8/FP8 KV cache quantization (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38378">#38378</a>), fused FP8/NVFP4 output quantization in MLA attention (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/35792">#35792</a>), NVFP4 dense models on MI300/MI355X and Hopper via emulation (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/35733">#35733</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Kernels</strong>: MXFP8 in Marlin GEMM/MoE with Mxfp8LinearOp refactor (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34664">#34664</a>), MXFP4 W4A4 CUTLASS MoE for SM100 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37463">#37463</a>), NVFP4 in <code>reshape_and_cache_flash</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37332">#37332</a>), batch-invariant NVFP4 linear (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39322">#39322</a>), FlashInfer CuteDSL batched-experts backend for NVFP4 MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38251">#38251</a>), special <code>GptOssMxfp4MoeMethod</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39604">#39604</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Compressed tensors</strong>: W8A8 MXFP8 linear/MoE (<code>CompressedTensorsW8A8Mxfp8</code>) (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38815">#38815</a>), layerwise reloading of attention/KV quantized models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38995">#38995</a>), experts_int8 consolidated with FP8 online quant (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38463">#38463</a>).</li>
<li><strong>XPU / CPU / AMD</strong>: XPU MXFP4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39857">#39857</a>), XPU MXFP8 GEMM + compressed-tensor schema (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38707">#38707</a>), XPU FP8 per-channel linear (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38316">#38316</a>), FP8 KV cache on XPU (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37731">#37731</a>), CPU W4A16 Autoround (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38192">#38192</a>), XPU W4A16 Autoround (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37986">#37986</a>), asymmetric INT8 <code>TritonInt8ScaledMMLinearKernel</code> on ROCm (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38501">#38501</a>), Quark W8A8 INT8 MoE inference (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/36320">#36320</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Deprecations</strong>: Petit NVFP4 removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32694">#32694</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>API &amp; Frontend</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>OpenAI API</strong>: <code>presence_penalty</code> / <code>frequency_penalty</code> on Responses API (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38613">#38613</a>), Responses API streaming migrated to unified parser (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38755">#38755</a>), Mistral Grammar factory (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38150">#38150</a>), multimodal support on <code>/inference/v1/generate</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38405">#38405</a>), <code>max_tokens_per_doc</code> in rerank (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38827">#38827</a>), Generative Scoring (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34539">#34539</a>), MaxSim re-enabled on GPU (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38620">#38620</a>), auto-detection of <code>reasoning_config</code> when only <code>reasoning_parser</code> is set (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38214">#38214</a>), reasoning parsers can access model config via <code>adjust_request</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37848">#37848</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Pooling ecosystem</strong>: Pooling entrypoints overhauled across scoring (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/28631">#28631</a>), pooling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39153">#39153</a>), and cleanup (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39675">#39675</a>); preprocessing/postprocessing offloaded to thread pool (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39763">#39763</a>); async scheduling disabled by default for pooling (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39592">#39592</a>); <code>logit_scale</code> added to PoolerConfig (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39435">#39435</a>), then renamed <code>logit_bias</code>/<code>logit_scale</code> → <code>logit_mean</code>/<code>logit_sigma</code> for affine score calibration (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39530">#39530</a>) — breaking.</li>
<li><strong>gRPC / streaming</strong>: Streaming on token-generation endpoint (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37171">#37171</a>); gRPC periodic stats logging + servicer log forwarding (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38333">#38333</a>).</li>
<li><strong>LLM / CLI</strong>: Structured-output special tokens preserved in offline <code>LLM.chat</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39352">#39352</a>), <code>use_audio_in_video</code> passable at <code>vllm serve</code> for nemotron-nano-vl (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38538">#38538</a>), deferred imports save ~2s CLI startup (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40056">#40056</a>), improved MM-input-too-long error message (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39409">#39409</a>), warning when FP8 KV cache misses prefill query quant (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39752">#39752</a>), clearer DCP error message (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/28443">#28443</a>), <code>--model</code> deprecation warning updated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39518">#39518</a>), Mimo reasoning/tooling parsers mapped (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/40089">#40089</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Security</h3>
<ul>
<li>SSRF fix in batch runner <code>download_bytes_from_url</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38482">#38482</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Dependencies</h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>PyTorch 2.11</strong> for CUDA (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/34644">#34644</a>); XPU torch-xpu reverted to 2.10 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39656">#39656</a>).</li>
<li><strong>CUDA 13.0</strong> default with updated architecture lists and cleaned build-args (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39878">#39878</a>).</li>
<li><strong>FlashAttention 4</strong> upstream sync (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38690">#38690</a>) and symlink-on-install behavior (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38814">#38814</a>).</li>
<li><strong>AITER</strong> triton BUFFER_OPS fix + version updates (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38580">#38580</a>), AITER reverted to v0.1.10.post3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39509">#39509</a>); <strong>Nixl</strong> bumped to 0.10.1 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39922">#39922</a>); <strong>DeepGEMM</strong> integrated into the wheel via CMake (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37980">#37980</a>); <strong>fastsafetensors</strong> added to NVIDIA Dockerfile (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38950">#38950</a>); Helion bumped 0.3.2 → 0.3.3 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38062">#38062</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Removed / moved</strong>: <code>resampy</code> dependency dropped (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39524">#39524</a>), <code>librosa</code> direct dependency dropped (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39079">#39079</a>), <code>pyav</code> and <code>soundfile</code> moved to common requirements (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39997">#39997</a>).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Breaking Changes</h3>
<ol>
<li><strong>PyTorch 2.11 + CUDA 13.0 default</strong> — environment dependency change.</li>
<li><strong>Metrics rework</strong>: <code>vllm:prompt_tokens_recomputed</code> removed (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38709">#38709</a>); <code>num_cached_tokens</code> / <code>num_external_computed_tokens</code> replaced with <code>PrefillStats</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/37460">#37460</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Pooler config rename</strong>: <code>logit_bias</code>/<code>logit_scale</code> → <code>logit_mean</code>/<code>logit_sigma</code> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39530">#39530</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Async scheduling default OFF for pooling models</strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39592">#39592</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Petit NVFP4 quantization removed</strong> (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32694">#32694</a>); <code>cprofile</code> / <code>cprofile_context</code> deprecated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39100">#39100</a>); V0 <code>accept output buffer</code> deprecated (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39125">#39125</a>).</li>
</ol>
<h3>V0 Deprecation</h3>
<ul>
<li>Petit NVFP4 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/32694">#32694</a>), accept output buffer in attention (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39125">#39125</a>), cprofile / cprofile_context (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39100">#39100</a>).</li>
</ul>

### [v0.19.1](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/releases/tag/v0.19.1)

**来源**: vLLM GitHub Releases

**摘要**: <p>This is a patch release on top of <code>v0.19.0</code> with Transformers v5.5.3 upgrade and bug fixes for Gemma4:</p>
<ul>
<li>Update to transformers v5 (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/30566">#30566</a>)</li>
<li>[Bugfix] Fix invalid JSON in Gemma 4 streaming tool calls by stripping partial delimiters (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38992">#38992</a>)</li>
<li>[Bugfix][Frontend] Fix Gemma4 streaming HTML duplication after tool calls (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38909">#38909</a>)</li>
<li>[Bugfix] Fix Gemma4 streaming tool call corruption for split boolean/number values (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39114">#39114</a>)</li>
<li>[Tool] adjust_request to reasoning parser, and Gemma4 fixes (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39027">#39027</a>)</li>
<li>[Gemma4] Support quantized MoE (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39045">#39045</a>)</li>
<li>Add Gemma4 Eagle3 support (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39450">#39450</a>)</li>
<li>[Gemma4][Bugfix]: Enable Gemma4ForCasualLM to load lora adapters correctly (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/38844">#38844</a>)</li>
<li>[Bugfix] Fix Gemma4 tool parser converting bare null to string "null" (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39679">#39679</a>)</li>
<li>[Model] Fix Gemma 4 token repetition by dynamic BOS injection for PT models (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39842">#39842</a>)</li>
<li>fix(kimi_k25): resolve media_placeholder_token_id from tokenizer (<a class="issue-link js-issue-link" href="https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/39344">#39344</a>)</li>
</ul>
